Autoresearch

The loop that tries to widen what this system can measure, and a record of what it was thinking while it did. Each iteration takes one claim the ledger cannot currently grade, proposes a single measurement for it, and code scores that proposal across every channel in the knowledge base. Nothing here changes a standing, a claim or the ledger: a kept proposal is a proposal, and the lever audit is still the gate.

The metric it is trying to move

The measurement frontier: how many live claims the ledger can actually grade. It rises only when a proposal reproduces across independent channels at the same bar a discovery has to clear, which is why it moves slowly and why it cannot be moved by loosening anything — the thresholds live in code the loop is not allowed to edit.

The bar is the shape of the problem: 1087 of 1118 live claims sit in the third band, which is the one this loop exists to shrink.

Declined as out of reach

A declined iteration is a RESULT, not a failure. Most of this knowledge base is about thumbnails, packaging and audience psychology, and the honest answer for those is that nothing in the vocabulary reaches them — the fix is more accessors, never a looser match.

Where the data pointed the other way

These did not become measurements, and that is the finding. Each is a relationship that reproduced across independent channels with a consistent sign and points AGAINST the claim it was proposed for. Nothing here changes a standing: refuting a claim is a human decision, and this is the evidence a person would weigh. Read each one with the same caution as a keep, since a proposal that measures the wrong thing can contradict a claim it never tested.

Retention and views are correlated but not causal — higher retention does not cause more views, so treating retention as a growth lever in isolation misattributes the mechanism.

hold-60s → views (claim predicts up)

20 channels · median -0.23 · 80% agree on the sign

the effect is real but points the other way: 1 of 20 readings held the claim's asserted direction

A universal average-percentage-viewed target is invalid because a high retention percentage can coexist with low demand — retention must be read as a curve against the channel's own comparable videos, not against a benchmark number.

apv → views (claim predicts null-result)

19 channels · median -0.24 · 89% agree on the sign

the claim predicts no reliable relationship, but the median effect is -0.24, which is a real one; the effect is real but points the other way: 6 of 19 readings held the claim's asserted direction

The standard fix for weak retention — cut the video shorter — is the wrong remedy; a retention problem in the back half is a setup problem in the opening, so the fix is better setup, not less runtime.

runtime → hold-90pct (claim predicts null-result)

19 channels · median -0.41 · 89% agree on the sign

the claim predicts no reliable relationship, but the median effect is -0.41, which is a real one; the effect is real but points the other way: 8 of 19 readings held the claim's asserted direction

Reproduced, but measuring something else

These cleared every evidence gate and were then rejected by the relevance audit: the relationship is real and the claim is not what it tests. They are shown because a loop that quietly dropped its own false positives would be a loop nobody could calibrate.

Average view duration and average percentage viewed can diverge sharply, and YouTube keeps recommending on the strength of AVD even at low APV - a 44-minute video with 20.4% APV kept being served because its AVD was 8:57.

avd-sec → impressions (up)

19 channels · median 0.37

The claim's central quantity is the DIVERGENCE between AVD and APV as a ranking signal — that serving tracks AVD even when APV is low — but APV appears nowhere in the pairing, so a one-sided AVD-vs-impressions rho cannot distinguish "served on AVD" from "served on APV or on general popularity"; worse, avd-sec is curve-mean times runtime and runtime varies across a catalogue by far more than the mean does, so the reading is substantially a runtime-vs-impressions relationship (and impressions are a downstream consequence of prior serving), which the proposer's own rationale concedes by writing off the claim's first half as "definitional and correctly unpairable".

A universal average-percentage-viewed target is invalid because a high retention percentage can coexist with low demand — retention must be read as a curve against the channel's own comparable videos, not against a benchmark number.

hold-60s → views (not-up)

19 channels · median -0.28

The claim's central quantity is average-percentage-viewed as a benchmark, but the probe measures hold-60s — a single first-minute sample of the curve, not its mean — and the proposer's own rationale concedes the substitution is a proxy "not APV itself", while the claim's real assertion (that a universal target is invalid and retention must be read within the channel's comparable set) is a prescription about the comparison basis rather than a hold-versus-demand correlation.

Longer videos carry a higher AVD baseline by construction, so a SHORTER video whose AVD matches or beats a longer one is genuine evidence of better retention — comparing raw AVD across lengths is invalid.

runtime → apv (down)

19 channels · median -0.44

The claim's central quantity is average view DURATION in seconds, which is absent from the vocabulary; apv is the curve mean, i.e. AVD divided by runtime, so pairing it with runtime is a structural artefact (runtime sits in the y-accessor's own denominator) and the proposer's rationale itself concedes it tests only the baseline, not the comparison rule.

A universal average-percentage-viewed target is invalid because a high retention percentage can coexist with low demand — retention must be read as a curve against the channel's own comparable videos, not against a benchmark number.

apv → views (differs)

19 channels · median -0.24

The claim asserts APV does NOT reliably predict demand, but direction "differs" is held by any substantial correlation of either sign (heldFor: |e| >= minEffect) while the frontier's own 0.2 effect floor discards the null the claim predicts, so the arithmetic passes equally for the claim and its negation and never reaches the claim's actual rule about reading the curve against comparable videos.

Kept proposals, awaiting a person

Each of these reproduced across independent channels and points the way its claim predicts. None is bound: binding is the lever audit's decision, and it rejects by default.

A very high retention chart combined with low views signals a wide-appeal problem: YouTube found the narrow audience that likes the video but believes nobody else will.

hold-60s → views (down)

20 channels · median -0.23 · 80% agree on the sign

This pairs the best available retention lever with views to test whether stronger early retention co-occurs with fewer views, the high-retention/low-view pattern attributed to narrow wide appeal.

A gap the vocabulary could not reach, and the probe built for it

One claim in the queue below counts independent creators, and no per-video accessor can express that: a figure about one video inside one channel cannot see across creators at all, and the loop said so rather than forcing a match. It has its own probe now, over the niche crawl instead of the channel store, and its unit is a niche rather than a channel. Shapes are mined from titles by the same statistics the format library uses, each creator is hidden in turn, and the question is whether a shape that worked for others also worked for them. Every figure is corrected against a shuffled null, because prediction improves with more evidence under pure noise. That correction is not enough on its own, and an adversarial audit proved it: shuffling destroys the differences between shapes, so the null cannot see an artefact that rides on them. The screen deciding which trials are asked therefore reads a FIXED number of creators whatever the evidence size, measured at 3% false positives on corpora where the claim is false by construction, down from 13% before.

Across 7 independent marketplaces and 1,396 leave-one-out trials, the effect is -0.023 at the median, 3 niches pointing one way and 4 the other. Each niche could have detected an effect of about 0.17, so an effect that size or larger is unlikely to be there. A smaller one would not have shown up here, and this cannot rule it out.

Nothing has resolved yet, and that is the reading. 7 of 7 niches produced a reading and 0 were decisive. An unresolved measurement is not written to the ledger, because an observation counts against a claim in its consistency score and a shrug is not evidence. Nothing was written, so the claim keeps the standing it had.

What the vocabulary cannot reach

Every claim the proposer declined, with the reason it gave. This is the most useful thing the loop produces: it is a build queue for the accessor vocabulary, written by something that has just tried to use it and found it short. An accessor is a code change with a test, so the queue is read by a person and never acted on by the loop.

Trying the deliberately bad or undesirable version ('I Tried a 1-Star Cruise', 'I Hired a 1-Star Stylist') outperforms trying the good version, because the audience wants to watch someone take a risk they would not take and expects drama and authentic reaction rather than a review.

The vocabulary has no lever for choosing a deliberately bad/undesirable version versus a good one; title-has-number is too loose because numbers can mark many non-bad formats.

87c74b75d7a0 · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Adding an emotional promise with a third-party verdict — the build must be approved by a specific person — raised both retention and views versus the same creator's earlier videos in the identical format, with the retention curve spiking at the reveal.

No variable in the vocabulary encodes the added emotional promise/third-party verdict or its reveal moment; format-run-depth only measures same-format repetition and cannot isolate that storytelling device.

19a509fd7031 · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Resolving the video's headline question before the end causes a large mid-video exodus — the drop is caused by the question being answered, not by the segment's quality.

The vocabulary has no lever measuring when a headline question is resolved, and title-is-question only marks whether the title is phrased as a question rather than whether it was answered early; so nothing here honestly tests this claim's own mechanism.

b53088cb6ada · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Foreshadowing the upcoming segment before entering it prevents segmentation loss — the loss occurs at the boundary when a viewer perceives a segment as complete, so segues must be phrased as continuations rather than conclusions.

The vocabulary has no variable measuring foreshadowed segues or segment-boundary phrasing, so there is no honest lever paired with a segmentation-loss outcome that tests this claim's own mechanism.

04b35695fede · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

A niche's ceiling is measurable in minutes: search the niche term on YouTube, filter out Shorts by video length, sort by most viewed, and read the top channels' typical view counts — that number is the total addressable market, and the gap to your own views is the real problem, not the niche.

The claim hinges on niche-specific search results, top-channel typical view counts as a ceiling, and the gap to your own views; none of those variables exist in the vocabulary. Runtime/views would only test whether longer videos generally get more views, not whether long-form top-viewed counts measure addressable market.

6752e9cd0f98 · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Packaging that worked during the first 24 hours when all your subscribers were rushing to watch might not be the same packaging you want to use long-term.

The claim requires comparing packaging performance in the first 24 hours with later discovery-window performance, but the vocabulary only has aggregate first-28-day outcomes like ctr and views, not an early-versus-later split.

70cc463adbfa · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Thumbnail carries the overwhelming share of the click decision and the title is secondary - Netflix measured artwork at over 80% of browsing attention with titles getting under two seconds.

The vocabulary lacks any thumbnail quality or attention metric (impressions only counts serves), so it cannot test whether artwork rather than title drives CTR.

95814f31ec16 · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

A 20% better title does not produce 20% more views — because YouTube's distribution is exponential and self-reinforcing, it can produce 10x or 100x more views, which is why title selection deserves disproportionate effort relative to its apparent size.

The vocabulary has no measure of title quality or a 20% title improvement, only surface title features; none honestly tests the claim's own variables.

ab6443eca979 · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Review and product/tool spotlight formats win clickability but lose replayability: they produce the channel's highest CTR and views while producing its LOWEST new-viewer-return rate and lowest APV — they buy an audience that does not come back.

The vocabulary lacks a variable identifying review/product/tool spotlight formats or new-viewer-return, so the format-specific CTR/APV tradeoff cannot be tested honestly; correlating ctr with apv would only test a general inverse relationship.

7ad2f833c056 · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

A compelling thumbnail concept is a VALIDATION TEST for a video idea: if you cannot invent a compelling thumbnail for an idea before production, the idea should not be made. This is stated identically in the appendix of all seven audits, making it house doctrine rather than channel-specific advice.

No vocabulary variable measures whether a compelling thumbnail concept was invented before production or its quality; the closest metrics like impressions and CTR are post-production exposure/performance proxies, not the claim's own pre-production validation lever.

f4cc0976876e · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Extreme weather as a subject broadens reach beyond a channel's niche because it is globally legible — it produced the channel's top-viewed video at 7.8% CTR in a motorcycle-travel niche.

No vocabulary variable marks a video as an extreme-weather subject or measures niche-broadening content, so the specific causal claim cannot be tested without inventing a topic metric.

26e14026c916 · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

A healthy returning-viewer share is 40–60% of total views; below roughly 30% the channel is acquisition-heavy and unstable, and near 50/50 it is approaching its current audience ceiling.

The vocabulary has no returning-viewer share or new/casual viewer split, so there is no honest pairing that tests this claim's own variable; engaged-share and TAM are only loose proxies.

9068ba73d783 · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

When a video's top retention moment occurs late, moving that kind of content earlier improves the video — and top moments should be expanded into entire new videos.

The vocabulary has no lever for moving a late top retention moment earlier or expanding it into new videos; biggest-rewind-position is outcome-only, and opening/title-delay variables are generic proxies rather than the claim's own variables.

07967f854da1 · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

A video with normal-or-good AVD/APV but low views is a PACKAGING failure, not a content failure, and the correct action is to repackage and relaunch rather than change how the video was made.

The claim is a conditional diagnosis (good APV/AVD plus low views means packaging failure) and prescribes repackaging, but the vocabulary lacks a direct packaging-quality/action variable and AVD/APV are outcomes rather than levers, so no bivariate lever-outcome correlation can test it honestly.

86a1d53c82f0 · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Swapping only the thumbnail for a more exaggerated version turned a video from 60,000 first-hour views into 3.9M views in three days, with zero change to audience retention and almost no viewer complaints about the exaggeration — proving thumbnail exaggeration lifts clicks without costing watch behaviour or trust.

The vocabulary has no lever measuring thumbnail exaggeration or a before/after thumbnail swap; CTR, views, and retention are downstream outcomes rather than the claimed packaging variable.

bc43db16d25e · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

A top-WTH video can have below-average APV

The claim's own variables are watch-time-hours and apv, both listed only as outcomes, while x must be a lever; the view-scale variable in the quote is also outcome-only, so no honest direct pairing exists.

ea0f9b715eb4 · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Attaching the current year to a title ('New Tax Rules for 2025 (MUST KNOW)') raises clicks by signalling freshness, and performs best at the start and end of the calendar year — meaning the same title has a seasonally varying value.

The vocabulary lacks a lever for whether the title contains the current year (title-has-number only flags any digit), and a single x-y correlation cannot test the claimed interaction between that packaging choice and publish-date seasonality.

85ff13b2b0c5 · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Returning viewers are manufactured by 'buckets' — near-identical formats with deliberately near-identical titles and metadata — because that similarity is what tells the algorithm whom to re-serve.

The claim requires a returning-viewer outcome, but the vocabulary has no metric for returning viewers; views and impressions are total-audience or serving proxies rather than honest tests of this specific variable.

a0a1b043f81c · theory · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Retention dips consistently at the END of build videos, and the fix is to hold a genuinely uncertain experimental payoff for the final segment and cut out mid-moment, so anticipation is never released before the video ends.

The claim’s key lever is a specific ending structure—holding an uncertain experimental payoff to the final segment and cutting mid-moment—which no variable in the vocabulary measures. The available levers are title, opening, runtime, format, or gap variables, so there is no honest pairing that tests this claim’s own mechanism.

4da98b83767d · law · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

The only fair cross-video comparison is each video's FIRST 28 DAYS measured from its own publish date — not lifetime views and not a fixed calendar window.

The vocabulary has no lifetime-view or fixed-calendar-window metric and no variable that encodes the first-28-days-from-publish-date comparison basis itself; a publish-date correlation with an early outcome would only test date trends, not whether that window is the fair cross-video basis.

4e090ab9fca5 · law · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

A fixed 'last N videos' analysis window is invalid across channels: 30 videos is 30 days for a daily uploader and three years for a monthly one, so the window must be normalised by upload cadence.

The claim requires comparing the calendar duration of a fixed video-count window across upload cadences, but the vocabulary lacks any variable for that span or for a cadence-normalized window; gap-before only proxies individual upload gaps and the outcomes are performance metrics unrelated to this methodological validity issue.

1e02ab9bdbff · law · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

A retention peak is not automatically a good premise — a peak caused by viewers skipping to a timestamp is different from a loved moment, and the discriminator is whether viewers keep watching after it.

The vocabulary has post-rewind-hold for whether viewers continue after the largest rise, but no lever variable represents a retention peak or its timestamp-skip versus loved-moment cause; with x required to be a lever there is no honest pairing that tests this claim's own variables.

f581b7301e4d · law · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

The more independent examples you find sharing the same angle of expectation — same viewer promise, different creators, different executions — the more likely it is a repeatable format rather than one creator's win. Around three convergent examples is usually enough to act on; more raises confidence rather than unlocking permission.

No variable counts independent examples sharing the same viewer promise across different creators, executions, and dates; format-run-depth only measures consecutive same-format uploads on the same channel.

9e642184ef76 · law · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp · a corpus probe now reaches this

How a sponsor read opens determines the drop: saying 'this video is sponsored by' or 'sign up', or starting with a negative undertone, directly causes retention drops during the read.

The vocabulary has no lever that captures whether a sponsor read says 'sign up', 'this video is sponsored by', or opens negatively; opening-words only counts words, so it cannot test this claim's own wording variable.

e0141dc7a94a · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Telling viewers to 'watch until the end' does not hold them, because it carries no information to be curious about; there is no data supporting it.

The vocabulary includes retention outcomes such as hold-half and apv, but it has no lever measuring whether the intro tells viewers to watch until the end, so this claim's own variable is not testable without inventing a metric.

f7dfa8a221b4 · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

The behaviour before and after the spike must be inspected before acting on it

The claim requires comparing behavior immediately before and after a retention spike, but the vocabulary has no pre-spike variable or action variable; post-rewind-hold alone cannot test that inspection is required.

b2f50720e4aa · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

A retention peak is not automatically a good premise, because a peak caused by viewers skipping to a timestamp is different from a loved moment

The claim requires distinguishing skip-induced retention peaks from loved moments and checking post-peak continuation; the vocabulary has post-rewind-hold but no allowed lever variable for the peak's cause or presence, so no honest x-y pairing tests this claim.

ec91f3e7ba45 · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Any type of repetition within a video — visual, narrative, verbal, musical, or editing, including restating the same meaning as subtext — tends to hurt retention; the exception is educational content where repetition aids memory.

The vocabulary has no lever measuring repetition inside a video (visual, narrative, verbal, musical, or editing) and no educational-content flag; format-run-depth measures repeated upload formats across videos, not the claimed within-video variable.

912702ff20ae · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Verbalizing assumptions about the viewer's thoughts ('you might be wondering...') creates a disconnect when the assumption is wrong and makes viewers start thinking instead of staying immersed.

The vocabulary has no lever measuring whether the creator verbalizes assumptions about viewer thoughts, so no honest pairing can test this specific immersion claim.

e64c16591f86 · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

A spike or dip spanning only one 1% data point may be a reporting artifact or a served ad; only features spanning multiple data points should be treated as real.

The claim is specifically about whether a retention spike or dip spans multiple 1% data points versus a single-point artifact, but the vocabulary has no variable measuring feature span, duration, or artifact status. Counts like flagged-drops or rewind-count only record how many moments occur and cannot distinguish multi-point real features from single-point noise.

efe8644d01de · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Reported impressions are unreliable (views divided by CTR does not reproduce them), so never optimize for impressions; optimize for an increase in views instead.

The claim requires comparing the derived quantity views divided by CTR to reported impressions, which a single two-variable correlation cannot test. No lever-outcome pair in the vocabulary honestly captures that reliability check without inventing a metric.

d33b4601c260 · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Any type of repetition within a video — visual, narrative, verbal, musical, or editing, including restating the same meaning as subtext — tends to hurt retention; the exception is educational content where repetition aids memory.

The vocabulary has no lever measuring repetition inside a video (visual, narrative, verbal, musical, or editing) and no educational-content flag; format-run-depth measures repeated upload formats across videos, not the claimed within-video variable.

912702ff20ae · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Verbalizing assumptions about the viewer's thoughts ('you might be wondering...') creates a disconnect when the assumption is wrong and makes viewers start thinking instead of staying immersed.

The vocabulary has no lever measuring whether the creator verbalizes assumptions about viewer thoughts, so no honest pairing can test this specific immersion claim.

e64c16591f86 · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Stated audience preference does not predict behaviour and can flatly contradict it — usage data must override survey and comment sentiment when the two disagree.

The vocabulary has no variable for stated audience preference, surveys, or comment sentiment, so it cannot honestly test whether stated preference contradicts usage behaviour.

df30b717c956 · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Originality is not the binding constraint for most creators — inexperienced work usually fails on the other six criteria (difficulty, relatability, believability, stakes, entertainment, meaning) while over-indexing on being different.

The vocabulary lacks any measure of script originality, the other six quality criteria, or writer experience; format-run-depth only proxies recent format repetition and would not test this claim's own variables.

1b71420b9852 · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

'Interesting' is not the same as 'entertaining' and only a small fraction of consumption is driven by interest — content that is merely informative underperforms content that produces a named emotional state.

The vocabulary contains no variable measuring whether a video is interesting, informative, or produces a named emotional state; title and opening features cannot distinguish those content qualities, so no honest pairing tests the claim.

2d71b6c17fc7 · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Content organised around 'succeeding at a work task' only holds an audience when the occupation is heroic, carries life-or-death stakes, and consists of high-emotion confrontations — otherwise the work must be backdrop for personal stakes.

The vocabulary has no variable for whether content centers on a work task, the occupation's heroism/life-or-death stakes, or personal-stakes framing; title/opening metrics are only loose proxies and cannot test this conditional claim.

b6c110e1fb00 · warning · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Fabricated stakes are detected and cost credibility — the risk in a video must be real either practically or emotionally.

No lever in the vocabulary measures whether a video's stakes are fabricated or authentic, so there is no honest pairing that tests this claim's own variables.

format-run-depth captures repetition of a shape, but the vocabulary has no outcome that measures whether repeated instances produce similar results or confidence in a format; performance metrics are single-video levels rather than similarity across the run.

A fixed 'last N videos' analysis window is invalid across channels: 30 videos is 30 days for a daily uploader and three years for a monthly one, so the window must be normalised by upload cadence.

The claim’s key variable is the calendar span of a fixed last-N-video window, which is not in the vocabulary; gap-before proxies cadence but no outcome measures that window’s duration or time-normalisation.

1e02ab9bdbff · law · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

A single video's retention spike is not evidence of enjoyment — it can be a rewatch, a share, a skip target, a surprising result, or a dense explanation, so the behaviour before and after the spike must be inspected before acting on it.

No valid pairing reaches the claim's own variables: retention-spike metrics are outcomes rather than levers, and there is no direct enjoyment metric in the vocabulary. Using title/runtime/opening levers would only be a loose proxy for interpreting spikes.

7604cadca891 · law · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

No lever in the vocabulary measures whether an uncertain experimental payoff is held for the final segment or cut mid-moment, and no variable identifies build-video endings; hold-90pct/apv can measure end retention but not this storytelling fix.

4da98b83767d · law · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

Pairing publish-date with views would only test age trend, not whether the first-28-day basis is fair.

4e090ab9fca5 · law · declined by qwen3.8-27b-nvfp4-mtp

AVD and APV normally move inversely (longer videos buy duration at the cost of percentage). When BOTH rise in the same window, that is the specific signature of genuine retention improvement, and it is the condition that licenses lengthening videos.

Declined. The first half of this claim is an identity, not an empirical relationship: average percentage viewed IS average view duration divided by runtime, so 'AVD and APV move inversely as videos get longer' is arithmetic rather than a finding, and every accessor that could express it (runtime, avd-sec, apv) shares the runtime series with the others. The same-series gate would reject the pairing and it should. The second half, that both rising together is the signature of genuine retention improvement, is a claim about a WINDOW moving over time, and every accessor here is a per-video figure with no before-and-after.

212c8f43726b · law · declined by opus-5 (file driver, 27-accessor vocabulary)

A retention drop must be measured as a share of the audience still present (relative drop), not as percentage points on the chart, so a 50%→40% fall is exactly as severe as 100%→80% and late drops that look small are routinely underweighted.

Declined. This is a rule about how a drop must be COMPUTED, not a claim that some trigger moves some metric. It asserts that a fall should be read as a share of the audience still present rather than as percentage points on the chart. There is no lever and no outcome to correlate; the way to check it is to read the hazard arithmetic in the retention analyser, which already implements exactly this, not to measure anything. Filing it as a gap would be misleading: the vocabulary is not short here, the claim simply is not of the shape a probe tests.

7f250a432f57 · law · declined by opus-5 (file driver, 27-accessor vocabulary)

A fixed 'last N videos' analysis window is invalid across channels: 30 videos is 30 days for a daily uploader and three years for a monthly one, so the window must be normalised by upload cadence.

Declined. The claim is that a fixed last-N-videos window is invalid across channels because N videos is a different span of time for a daily uploader than for a monthly one. That is a statement about the comparability of an ANALYSIS WINDOW, and the unit it speaks about is a channel, not a video. gap-before reads days since the previous upload per video and could show that cadence varies enormously between channels, but demonstrating that variance exists is not the same as testing whether a window built on it is invalid. What would reach this is a probe whose unit is a channel and whose outcome is the stability of a figure under two different windowing rules.

1e02ab9bdbff · law · declined by opus-5 (file driver, 27-accessor vocabulary)

There is no universal CTR target: half of all channels and videos fall anywhere between 2% and 10%, and low-view or new videos vary more widely, so a fixed threshold produces false diagnoses and invites clickbait.

Declined, and worth naming precisely because the data IS present. The claim is that no universal click-through target exists, since half of all channels and videos land anywhere between 2% and 10%. That is a claim about the SPREAD of a single accessor, not about a relationship between two, and every probe in this battery correlates a pair. ctr is populated from the Studio pull, so the check is available and cheap; it needs a probe shape that reports a distribution and its interquartile range rather than a correlation.

2e43d71ef393 · warning · declined by opus-5 (file driver, 27-accessor vocabulary)

Upload consistency matters far less once traffic comes from search or recommendations - it primarily matters for subscription-fed channels and for producing comparable metrics.

Declined. The claim is conditional on traffic source: upload consistency matters far less once views arrive from search or recommendations, and primarily matters for subscription-fed channels. gap-before against views would test whether consistency matters at all, which is a different and weaker question, and a null reading would be unattributable between 'consistency does not matter' and 'these channels are search-fed'. Nothing in this vocabulary reads where a view came from. A traffic-source accessor is the missing piece, and it is available from both the Studio pull and the public Analytics API.

3d7710fb7636 · warning · declined by opus-5 (file driver, 27-accessor vocabulary)

Packaging outweighs retention as a driver of views by a very large margin: a high-retention video with weak packaging can be dwarfed by a low-retention video with strong packaging. Part of this is the system itself — YouTube recommends to people it predicts will click, so an extremely niche premise may barely be pushed at all.

Declined as a single pairing. The claim is COMPARATIVE: packaging outweighs retention as a driver of views by a large margin. Testing it needs two correlations measured on the same videos, ctr against views and hold-half against views, and then a comparison of their strengths. Each half is individually proposable and the comparison is the claim, so scoring either half alone and recording it against this claim would file a partial answer as a whole one. What would reach it is a probe that takes two pairings and reports the difference between them, which is a shape this battery does not have.

63223d2e9878 · law · declined by opus-5 (file driver, 27-accessor vocabulary)

An early retention dip is NOT automatically a content defect — notifications, chapter-skipping, mismatched traffic sources and accidental autoplay all distort the curve, so the dip must be matched to the exact footage and script before diagnosis.

Declined. The claim is that an early retention dip is not automatically a content defect, because notifications, chapter-skipping, mismatched traffic sources and accidental autoplay all distort the opening of the curve. Every one of those alternative causes is unobservable here: no accessor reads traffic source, chapter markers, notification delivery or autoplay. hold-30s would measure the dip and say nothing about its cause, and a probe that measured the dip while the claim is about its ATTRIBUTION would look like a test and not be one.

b0870674306d · warning · declined by opus-5 (file driver, 27-accessor vocabulary)

Retention dips consistently at the END of build videos, and the fix is to hold a genuinely uncertain experimental payoff for the final segment and cut out mid-moment, so anticipation is never released before the video ends.

The lever is an editorial choice about the final segment — holding a genuinely uncertain payoff back and cutting out mid-moment — and nothing observes it; the widening did not help, because the outcome it predicts (end retention) lives only in curve accessors, and any curve-vs-curve pairing is refused as one series' own arithmetic. biggest-rewind-position would be a stretch: a late rise can equally be a skip landing or an outro artefact, and the claim never asserts that late peaks earn views.

4da98b83767d · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max, 27-accessor vocabulary)

The only fair cross-video comparison is each video's FIRST 28 DAYS measured from its own publish date — not lifetime views and not a fixed calendar window.

This prescribes the BASIS on which views are counted — a property of the comparison set, not a relationship between two per-video variables. The views accessor already arrives on the set's own chosen basis and scoreSpec age-normalises every context, so any pairing would grade the pipeline's own windowing convention rather than the claim, and publish-date-vs-views is refused as recency-vs-views in any case.

4e090ab9fca5 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max, 27-accessor vocabulary)

Longer videos carry a higher AVD baseline by construction, so a SHORTER video whose AVD matches or beats a longer one is genuine evidence of better retention — comparing raw AVD across lengths is invalid.

avd-sec now exists but carries ['curve','runtime'], so the pair the claim literally names — runtime against AVD — is refused, and the refusal is right: AVD is the curve mean times runtime, so runtime sits inside the y-accessor, and 'longer videos carry a higher AVD baseline by construction' is a definition rather than a finding. The second half (a shorter video matching a longer one's AVD is better retention) is the same arithmetic read backwards, i.e. APV, which puts runtime in the denominator again.

d08914b51d07 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max, 27-accessor vocabulary)

A single video's retention spike is not evidence of enjoyment — it can be a rewatch, a share, a skip target, a surprising result, or a dense explanation, so the behaviour before and after the spike must be inspected before acting on it.

The claim needs the behaviour BEFORE and AFTER one spike plus a way to separate five named causes (rewatch, share, skip target, surprising result, dense explanation); nothing here attributes a cause, and no accessor reads the neighbourhood before a rise. The one accessor that reaches the 'after' half, post-rewind-hold, is the honest home of f581b7301e4d, and binding both would file a single arithmetic as two independent measurements — the double-count the last round flagged.

7604cadca891 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max, 27-accessor vocabulary)

Nothing in a per-video pairing can express convergence across creators.

9e642184ef76 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max, 27-accessor vocabulary) · a corpus probe now reaches this

A single winner does not establish a format — the win may belong to the subject, the guest, a timing event or one package rather than the shape. Repetition is what proves it: the more times the same shape produces a similar result, the more confidently it can be called a format.

The claim is about the CONSISTENCY of a shape's results across repetitions — variance within a format — and about how much repetition licenses calling it a format; no accessor measures within-format variance, and format-run-depth measures back-to-back sameness, a different construct already spoken for by consecutive-same-format probes.

0cf8b8fc71fe · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max, 27-accessor vocabulary)

Retention curves are not directly comparable across channels — each audience has its own viewing habits, so the primary benchmark must be channel-internal. Cross-channel comparison is still useful once the variables are aligned (similar length, format and audience composition); it is weaker evidence, not worthless evidence.

This is an assertion about BETWEEN-channel variance in curve shape, which needs a variance decomposition, not a rank correlation of two per-video accessors. Cross-channel disagreement is a property scoreSpec computes about every probe (signAgreement), not something a proposal can put on either axis, and a claim is not tested by designing a probe to fail.

90ab4955b236 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max, 27-accessor vocabulary)

Any viewer lost in the first minute is mathematically excluded from finishing, so first-minute retention caps AVD — which justifies spending ~25% of total production time on the first minute alone.

The first half is arithmetic — a viewer lost at 60s cannot be in the audience later — so hold-60s against avd-sec or apv is the same curve on both sides and is refused, correctly, as the nesting the judge already threw out. The second half prescribes spending ~25% of production time on the first minute, and production effort is not in the data at all.

3bbd57437810 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max, 27-accessor vocabulary)

Raising CTR and AVD in isolation may not raise views at all — the funnel is the object of optimisation, not either metric individually.

Its CTR leg is already measured as ctr-vs-views and is refused here for that reason, and its AVD leg would restate the apv-vs-views arithmetic proposed for d559fa89a10e, differing only by a runtime factor whose own effect the battery measures separately — so a null there could be two real effects cancelling. What is distinctive about the claim, that the funnel must be optimised jointly rather than either metric alone, is not a pairwise relationship at all.

8f41c03b8963 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max, 27-accessor vocabulary)

Retention dips consistently at the END of build videos, and the fix is to hold a genuinely uncertain experimental payoff for the final segment and cut out mid-moment, so anticipation is never released before the video ends.

The claim's variable is a structural choice about the ENDING — whether a genuinely uncertain payoff is held back and cut mid-moment — and nothing in the vocabulary encodes anything about what happens late in a video's construction. hold-90pct exists as an outcome but has no honest predictor here, so any pairing would be an unrelated x standing in for the claim's actual lever.

4da98b83767d · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max)

A retention chart that is high and almost flat is a WIDE-APPEAL failure, not a success: it means YouTube found only the core audience that already loves the format, and the fix is broadening appeal rather than any retention edit.

The claim identifies a specific curve SHAPE — high and almost flat — and no left-eligible accessor measures flatness; the only whole-curve measures (hold-90pct, apv) are right-only. Pairing a first-minute hold against views would test retention LEVEL and would sweep in exactly the steep-drop videos the claim excludes, so it would grade a different population than the one described.

d08914b51d07 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max)

7604cadca891 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max)

f581b7301e4d · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max)

9e642184ef76 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max) · a corpus probe now reaches this

0cf8b8fc71fe · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max)

Average view duration and average percentage viewed can diverge sharply, and YouTube keeps recommending on the strength of AVD even at low APV - a 44-minute video with 20.4% APV kept being served because its AVD was 8:57.

Both variables in the central assertion are AVD and its divergence from APV, and there is no AVD accessor. apv exists but is right-only, so the divergence cannot even be placed on the predictor side, and the algorithmic-serving half needs AVD too.

72184fe7ce69 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max)

Retention curves are not directly comparable across channels — each audience has its own viewing habits, so the primary benchmark must be channel-internal. Cross-channel comparison is still useful once the variables are aligned (similar length, format and audience composition); it is weaker evidence, not worthless evidence.

This is a statement about between-channel variance in curve shape; settling it needs a variance decomposition across channels, not a rank correlation of two per-video accessors. No pairing in this vocabulary can express 'these curves are not comparable to each other'.

90ab4955b236 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: max)

Retention dips consistently at the END of build videos, and the fix is to hold a genuinely uncertain experimental payoff for the final segment and cut out mid-moment, so anticipation is never released before the video ends.

The claim is about where an uncertain payoff is placed within the video's structure; the vocabulary has no accessor for payoff placement, end-segment structure, or cut-out timing. hold-90pct alone tests nothing about the editorial choice.

4da98b83767d · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: low)

A retention chart that is high and almost flat is a WIDE-APPEAL failure, not a success: it means YouTube found only the core audience that already loves the format, and the fix is broadening appeal rather than any retention edit.

Requires a measure of curve flatness (variance across the retention curve) plus a reach/impressions signal; the vocabulary offers only point holds, and no flatness or audience-breadth accessor exists.

6622c60c167d · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: low)

The only fair cross-video comparison is each video's FIRST 28 DAYS measured from its own publish date — not lifetime views and not a fixed calendar window.

This is a methodological claim about the correct views basis, not a per-video relationship between two accessors. No pairing can test it.

4e090ab9fca5 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: low)

A single video's retention spike is not evidence of enjoyment — it can be a rewatch, a share, a skip target, a surprising result, or a dense explanation, so the behaviour before and after the spike must be inspected before acting on it.

A claim about interpreting individual spikes and inspecting behaviour around them; nothing in the vocabulary describes spikes or their neighbourhoods (flagged-drops counts drops, not peaks).

7604cadca891 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: low)

A retention peak is not automatically a good premise — a peak caused by viewers skipping to a timestamp is different from a loved moment, and the discriminator is whether viewers keep watching after it.

Needs per-peak measurement of whether viewers continue watching after a rise. The vocabulary has no peak accessor and no post-moment continuation measure.

f581b7301e4d · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: low)

The more independent examples you find sharing the same angle of expectation — same viewer promise, different creators, different executions — the more likely it is a repeatable format rather than one creator's win. Around three convergent examples is usually enough to act on; more raises confidence rather than unlocking permission.

About convergent evidence across creators for a format concept; no accessor counts independent cross-creator examples, and format-run-depth is within-channel repetition, a different variable.

9e642184ef76 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: low) · a corpus probe now reaches this

A single winner does not establish a format — the win may belong to the subject, the guest, a timing event or one package rather than the shape. Repetition is what proves it: the more times the same shape produces a similar result, the more confidently it can be called a format.

About whether a repeated shape reproduces a result — a question about variance across repetitions of a format, not a monotone per-video pairing. format-run-depth measures back-to-back sameness, which the knowledge base already distinguishes from format proof.

0cf8b8fc71fe · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: low)

Retention curves are not directly comparable across channels — each audience has its own viewing habits, so the primary benchmark must be channel-internal. Cross-channel comparison is still useful once the variables are aligned (similar length, format and audience composition); it is weaker evidence, not worthless evidence.

A claim about the validity of cross-channel comparison of curves — a statement about the statistic's own basis, not a per-video x/y relationship measurable within the vocabulary.

90ab4955b236 · law · declined by opus-5 (effort: low)

1 not run runtime → avd-sec (up) Longer videos carry a higher AVD baseline by construction, so a SHORTER video whose AVD matches…

Claim d08914b51d07 · law · Longer videos carry a higher AVD baseline by construction, so a SHORTER video whose AVD matches or beats a longer one is genuine evidence of better retention — comparing raw AVD across lengths is invalid.

What it was thinking: The claim's testable premise is that raw average view duration rises mechanically with video length, so across channels runtime should positively correlate with AVD seconds; a short video matching a longer one would then signal stronger retention.

What the code decided: both are computed from the same runtime, so a correlation between them measures that series' own arithmetic rather than a relationship anyone could act on

What it was thinking: The claim is about choosing a measurement window (first 28 days from each video's own publish date) rather than lifetime or fixed-calendar views, but the vocabulary has no variable for alternative windows or a fairness/validity outcome. Pairing publish-date with views would only test age trend, not whether the first-28-day basis is fair.

4 not run runtime → avd-sec (up) Longer videos carry a higher AVD baseline by construction, so a SHORTER video whose AVD matches…

Claim d08914b51d07 · law · Longer videos carry a higher AVD baseline by construction, so a SHORTER video whose AVD matches or beats a longer one is genuine evidence of better retention — comparing raw AVD across lengths is invalid.

What it was thinking: The claim's testable premise is that raw average view duration rises mechanically with video length, so across channels runtime should positively correlate with AVD seconds; a short video matching a longer one would then signal stronger retention.

What the code decided: both are computed from the same runtime, so a correlation between them measures that series' own arithmetic rather than a relationship anyone could act on

3 discarded ctr → hold-half (down) 'Payoff debt' — the package creating a larger question than the video resolves — is a distinct …

Claim 571e8b5eb24c · warning · 'Payoff debt' — the package creating a larger question than the video resolves — is a distinct failure from a weak idea, and shows up as satisfied clicks with unsatisfied viewers.

What it was thinking: Payoff debt is defined as satisfied clicks with unsatisfied viewers: the package promises a larger question than the video answers. At population level that predicts a negative relationship between click-through and how much of the audience is still there at the halfway mark. 6 not run no proposal AVD and APV normally move inversely (longer videos buy duration at the cost of percentage). Whe…

Claim 212c8f43726b · law · AVD and APV normally move inversely (longer videos buy duration at the cost of percentage). When BOTH rise in the same window, that is the specific signature of genuine retention improvement, and it is the condition that licenses lengthening videos.

What it was thinking: Declined. The first half of this claim is an identity, not an empirical relationship: average percentage viewed IS average view duration divided by runtime, so 'AVD and APV move inversely as videos get longer' is arithmetic rather than a finding, and every accessor that could express it (runtime, avd-sec, apv) shares the runtime series with the others. The same-series gate would reject the pairing and it should. The second half, that both rising together is the signature of genuine retention improvement, is a claim about a WINDOW moving over time, and every accessor here is a per-video figure with no before-and-after.

10 kept avd-sec → impressions (up) Average view duration and average percentage viewed can diverge sharply, and YouTube keeps reco…

Claim 72184fe7ce69 · law · Average view duration and average percentage viewed can diverge sharply, and YouTube keeps recommending on the strength of AVD even at low APV - a 44-minute video with 20.4% APV kept being served because its AVD was 8:57.

What it was thinking: The claim's testable half is that YouTube keeps SERVING on the strength of AVD, and impressions is literally how many times the thumbnail was served, so this is the claim's own mechanism in its own units. That avd-sec carries runtime is not a confound here but the claim's point — its example is a 44-minute video whose length inflated AVD to 8:57 at 20.4% APV and which kept being served anyway; the first half, that AVD and APV diverge, is definitional and correctly unpairable.

What the code decided: reproduced on 19 channels at a median 0.37, 89% agreeing on the sign, 12 holding the asserted direction

Relevance audit: REJECTED — measures something the claim does not assert · The claim's central quantity is the DIVERGENCE between AVD and APV as a ranking signal — that serving tracks AVD even when APV is low — but APV appears nowhere in the pairing, so a one-sided AVD-vs-impressions rho cannot distinguish "served on AVD" from "served on APV or on general popularity"; worse, avd-sec is curve-mean times runtime and runtime varies across a catalogue by far more than the mean does, so the reading is substantially a runtime-vs-impressions relationship (and impressions are a downstream consequence of prior serving), which the proposer's own rationale concedes by writing off the claim's first half as "definitional and correctly unpairable". 13 not run hold-30s → hold-90pct (up) The standard fix for weak retention — cut the video shorter — is the wrong remedy; a retention …

Claim 78c39cd4325b · law · The standard fix for weak retention — cut the video shorter — is the wrong remedy; a retention problem in the back half is a setup problem in the opening, so the fix is better setup, not less runtime.

What it was thinking: The claim's causal core is that a back-half retention problem originates in the opening, so how well act one holds should predict how much audience survives deep into the video. The two are nested (hold-90pct cannot exceed hold-30s), which is a ceiling rather than a determination, so this is informative but weaker than it looks; the 'cutting shorter is the wrong fix' half is deliberately left unbound because runtime-vs-apv is confounded by the length artefact and would falsely refute.

What the code decided: both are computed from the same retention curve, so a correlation between them measures that series' own arithmetic rather than a relationship anyone could act on

4 kept runtime → apv (down) Longer videos carry a higher AVD baseline by construction, so a SHORTER video whose AVD matches…

Claim d08914b51d07 · law · Longer videos carry a higher AVD baseline by construction, so a SHORTER video whose AVD matches or beats a longer one is genuine evidence of better retention — comparing raw AVD across lengths is invalid.

What it was thinking: The claim's mechanism is that AVD rises with runtime by construction, which is equivalent to APV falling as runtime grows; runtime vs apv tests exactly that length-baseline relationship. It does not test the comparison rule itself, only the baseline it rests on.

What the code decided: reproduced on 19 channels at a median -0.44, 95% agreeing on the sign, 17 holding the asserted direction

Relevance audit: REJECTED — measures something the claim does not assert · The claim's central quantity is average view DURATION in seconds, which is absent from the vocabulary; apv is the curve mean, i.e. AVD divided by runtime, so pairing it with runtime is a structural artefact (runtime sits in the y-accessor's own denominator) and the proposer's rationale itself concedes it tests only the baseline, not the comparison rule. (opus-5 (effort: high))
